# Luma AMD Speedrun: Phase 9 — Team-Parallel Kernel Optimization

## Context

**Phase 8 is complete.** Three kernels are on the leaderboard. Significant gaps remain.

| Kernel | Our Time | Leader | Gap | Current Approach | Bottleneck |
|--------|----------|--------|-----|-----------------|------------|
| MLA | ~129 µs | 4.3 µs | **30x** | FlashDecoding + hybrid routing (threshold=100k) | VGPR pressure (kv1[64,512]=256 VGPRs); bf16 bandwidth |
| GEMM | ~20.6 µs | 9.7 µs | **2.1x** | `gemm_afp4wfp4` (Triton) | API ceiling; gemm_a4w4 blocked by callsite sensitivity |
| MoE | ~155 µs | 145 µs | **1.07x** | Adaptive KSPLIT routing | API ceiling |

**Phase 8 key learnings** (skills: `gfx950-vgpr-pressure-triton-mla`, `amd-triton-jit-callsite-correctness`):
1. VGPR pressure from KV tile, not accumulator — BLOCK_M tiling didn't help
2. BLOCK_M tiling increases KV bandwidth (nq/BLOCK_M independent KV loads)
3. ref_kernel has ~130 µs fixed overhead (metadata + JIT dispatch)
4. gemm_a4w4 HIP ASM produces ~1-3% different results from submission.py callsite
5. For shapes under 100k tokens, kernel launch + Python overhead dominate

**Why a team approach:** Phase 8 was sequential — one agent doing MLA, then GEMM, then MoE.
The three kernels are independent (different files, leaderboards, and optimization strategies).
A team can parallelize research and code writing while the lead handles sequential submissions.

## Team Architecture

**Team name:** `kernel-speedrun`

| Agent | Role | File Ownership | Tools Needed |
|-------|------|----------------|-------------|
| **Lead** (main session) | Coordinates, submits via popcorn-cli, reviews results | None (reads all) | Bash (popcorn-cli), SendMessage |
| **mla-dev** | MLA kernel research + implementation | `kernels/mixed-mla/` | Read, Write, Edit, Grep, Bash |
| **gemm-dev** | GEMM kernel research + implementation | `kernels/mxfp4-mm/` | Read, Write, Edit, Grep, Bash |

**Why 3 agents, not more:**
- Only 2 kernels have meaningful improvement headroom (MoE is at 1.07x gap — diminishing returns)
- Popcorn-cli submissions are sequential (~10 min each) — the lead is the bottleneck
- More agents = more coordination overhead + linear token cost
- 3 is the sweet spot: 2 implementers saturate the submission pipeline

**Why not a dedicated research agent:**
- Each kernel's research is highly specialized (MLA: attention algorithms; GEMM: quantized matmul)
- A generalist researcher would duplicate context that kernel-specific agents already need
- Each dev agent does targeted research within their domain

### Communication Protocol

```
mla-dev ──writes code──→ kernels/mixed-mla/submission.py
         ──messages────→ Lead: "ready for submission"

gemm-dev ──writes code─→ kernels/mxfp4-mm/submission.py
          ──messages───→ Lead: "ready for submission"

Lead ──submits──→ popcorn-cli (sequential, ~10 min per submit)
     ──messages─→ mla-dev/gemm-dev: "results: X µs, errors: Y"
```

**Submission queue:** Lead maintains a FIFO queue. While waiting for one submission,
the other agent can be writing code. Typical cycle:
1. mla-dev signals ready → Lead submits MLA → 10 min wait
2. During wait, gemm-dev signals ready → Lead queues GEMM
3. MLA result arrives → Lead messages mla-dev → Lead submits GEMM
4. While GEMM runs, mla-dev iterates based on results

- `kernels/mxfp4-mm/reference.py` — aiter gemm_a4w4 reference

- [ ] Task 7: Explore CK direct GEMM — check if Composable Kernel has a direct fp4 GEMM callable from Python

### Shared

**Total Tasks:** 8 | **Done:** 0 | **Left:** 8

## Task Details

### Task 1: MLA — FP8 Score GEMM (mla-dev)

**Why:** bf16 tl.dot uses half the MFMA throughput of fp8. For compute-bound shapes (bs≤32),
fp8 scoring gives ~1.5-2x speedup. The input already provides `kv_data["fp8"]`.

**Approach:**
1. Read `reference.py:quantize_fp8()` for Q quantization pattern
2. Inside `_mla_flash_decode` kernel: load fp8 Q + fp8 KV tiles
3. `qk = tl.dot(q1_fp8, tl.trans(kv1_fp8))` with fp32 accumulator
4. Apply `q_scale * kv_scale * SM_SCALE` after the dot product
5. Keep value accumulation in bf16 initially (Task 2 upgrades this)

**Constraint:** `tl.dot` with fp8 requires BLOCK_M ≥ 16 and BLOCK_N ≥ 16 on gfx950.
Current BLOCK_M=16, BLOCK_N=64 — both satisfy this.

### Task 2: MLA — FP8 Value Path (mla-dev)

**Why:** For bandwidth-bound shapes (bs=256, kv=8k), KV bandwidth dominates at ~288 µs (bf16).
FP8 KV halves this to ~144 µs. Using fp8 for BOTH score AND value means only ONE KV load.

**Approach:**
1. Load fp8 KV once per tile (instead of bf16 KV for values)
2. Score: `tl.dot(q_fp8, tl.trans(kv_fp8))` — already done in Task 1
3. Value: `tl.dot(p.to(fp8), kv1_fp8)` — accumulate in fp32, scale at end
4. Final output: `acc * kv_scale` → bf16

**Risk:** FP8 value accumulation may exceed rtol=1e-2 tolerance. Mitigation: if fp8 value
fails correctness, keep bf16 value path and only use fp8 for score (Task 1 alone).

### Task 3: MLA — BLOCK_N Tuning (mla-dev)

**Why:** The real VGPR bottleneck is `kv1[BLOCK_N, 512]`. At BLOCK_N=64: ~256 VGPRs (saturates budget).
At BLOCK_N=32: ~128 VGPRs (fits with accumulator). Smaller BLOCK_N trades tile efficiency for
register pressure — may eliminate spilling and improve throughput.

**Approach:**
1. Parameterize BLOCK_N as a tunable constexpr
2. Test BLOCK_N ∈ {32, 64, 128} via benchmark
3. Smaller BLOCK_N = more loop iterations but less spilling
4. Larger BLOCK_N = fewer iterations but more register pressure

### Task 4: MLA — Threshold Sweep (mla-dev)

**Why:** Current threshold=100k was set empirically in Phase 8. With fp8 and BLOCK_N changes,
the optimal threshold shifts. Need to re-benchmark.

**Approach:** After Tasks 1-3, benchmark all shapes with FlashDecoding forced on vs ref_kernel.
Set threshold at the crossover point.

### Task 5: GEMM — Fix Fused Kernel (gemm-dev)

**Why:** `submission_tritonblas_fused.py` has the Origami XCD remapping bug (non-bijective for
`total_tiles % 8 != 0`). Fixing this with group-M swizzle could unlock the fused kernel path,
eliminating ~10 µs of quant + launch overhead.

**Approach:**
1. Copy `submission_tritonblas_fused.py` → working copy
2. Replace Origami XCD block with group-M swizzle (see `tritonblas-origami-xcd-remapping-bug` skill)
3. Test correctness across all 5 GEMM shapes
4. If passes: benchmark and compare against 20.6 µs baseline

### Task 6: GEMM — CUDA Graph Wrapping (gemm-dev)

**Why:** Our ~10 µs overhead per shape = A quant launch + GEMM launch + allocation.
CUDA graphs capture the entire sequence and replay with ~2-3 µs overhead.

**Approach:**
1. Wrap `dynamic_mxfp4_quant` + `gemm_afp4wfp4` in a CUDA graph
2. Use `torch.cuda.CUDAGraph()` with warmup + capture + replay
3. Challenge: dynamic shapes require separate graphs per shape (5 shapes = 5 graphs)
4. Pre-warm all 5 during first invocation

**Risk:** CUDA graph capture may not work with aiter's Triton kernels (JIT compilation
happens at capture time, not replay). Test empirically.

### Task 7: GEMM — Explore CK Direct GEMM (gemm-dev)

**Why:** If neither fused kernel nor CUDA graphs work, explore Composable Kernel (CK)
for a direct fp4 GEMM path that bypasses both aiter and Triton overhead.

**Approach:**
1. Check if `ck` or `composable_kernel` is importable on MI355X runner
2. Search for fp4/mxfp4 GEMM kernels in CK Python bindings
3. If found: write a minimal submission using CK directly

### Task 8: Final Submissions (Lead)

Submit best results from both tracks to leaderboard.

## Execution Flow (Team-Parallel)

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ Lead: TeamCreate("kernel-speedrun")                      │
│ Lead: Create tasks, spawn mla-dev + gemm-dev             │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│                                                          │
│  mla-dev (parallel)          gemm-dev (parallel)         │
│  ┌──────────────────┐       ┌──────────────────┐        │
│  │ Task 1: fp8 score │       │ Task 5: fix fused │        │
│  │ (read ref, write  │       │ (fix XCD, test    │        │
│  │  kernel, signal)  │       │  locally, signal)  │        │
│  └────────┬─────────┘       └────────┬─────────┘        │
│           │                           │                   │
│  Lead: submit MLA ◄──────────────────┘ Lead: submit GEMM │
│  (10 min wait)                         (queued)           │
│           │                                               │
│  mla-dev: iterate   ◄── Lead: "MLA result: X µs"         │
│  ┌──────────────────┐                                    │
│  │ Task 2: fp8 value │       gemm-dev: iterate            │
│  │ Task 3: BLOCK_N   │       ┌──────────────────┐        │
│  └────────┬─────────┘       │ Task 6: CUDA graph │        │
│           │                  │ Task 7: CK explore │        │
│           ▼                  └────────┬─────────┘        │
│  Lead: submit best MLA                │                   │
│  Lead: submit best GEMM ◄────────────┘                   │
│                                                          │
│  Task 8: Final leaderboard submissions                   │
│  Lead: TeamDelete("kernel-speedrun")                     │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Efficiency gains over sequential Phase 8:**
- Research + code writing happens in parallel (2 agents × ~30 min = 60 min of parallel work)
- Submission pipeline stays saturated (one kernel submitting while other iterates)
- Estimated: 4-6 submission cycles in ~60 min vs 2-3 sequential

| CK not available on runner | Task 7 fails | Accept API ceiling |
| Agent coordination overhead | Slow iteration | Keep team small (3 agents) |
| Edit conflicts on same file | Corruption | Strict file ownership per agent |

# Popcorn CLI AMD Kernel Submission Workflow

## Submission Commands

```bash
CLI=~/.local/bin/popcorn-cli
KERNELS=/path/to/kernels/dir

# 1. Test (correctness check — do this first)
$CLI submit --no-tui --mode test --gpu MI355X --leaderboard <name> $KERNELS/<kernel>/submission.py

# 2. Benchmark (timing, no leaderboard impact)
$CLI submit --no-tui --mode benchmark --gpu MI355X --leaderboard <name> $KERNELS/<kernel>/submission.py

# 3. Leaderboard (official ranked submission)
$CLI submit --no-tui --mode leaderboard --gpu MI355X --leaderboard <name> $KERNELS/<kernel>/submission.py
```

## Leaderboard Name Pattern

Leaderboard names follow the pattern: `amd-<directory-name>`

| Kernel directory | Leaderboard name |
|-----------------|-----------------|
| `mxfp4-mm/`    | `amd-mxfp4-mm`  |
| `mixed-mla/`   | `amd-mixed-mla` |
| `moe-mxfp4/`   | `amd-moe-mxfp4` |

To discover unknown names: submit with `--mode test` using candidate names. A 404/does-not-exist
response means the name is wrong; acceptance (even with correctness failures) confirms the name.

## Submission Limits (Luma AMD Speedrun)

- **No per-user rate limit** — submit as many times as needed
- Server-wide: 3 concurrent submissions (asyncio.Semaphore)
- ~10 requests/second global rate limit
- Per-mode timeouts: test=180s, benchmark=180s (overridden per competition in task.yml)
- **Total workflow timeout: ~12 minutes** — the entire GitHub Actions run must complete within this
- Use `--mode test` first, then `--mode benchmark`, then `--mode leaderboard`

## aiter JIT Compilation Times (Luma AMD Speedrun)

aiter builds JIT modules on first use. These builds happen **inside** the 12-minute workflow timeout:

| JIT Module | Build Time |
|-----------|-----------|
| `module_moe_sorting` | ~25s |
| `module_moe_ck2stages_fp4x2_*` | ~103s |
| `module_mla_metadata` | ~32s |
| `module_mla_asm` | ~21s |
| `module_mla_reduce` | ~171s |

**Critical implications:**
- MoE JIT builds alone take ~230s (~4 min), leaving only ~8 min for actual execution
- MLA JIT builds take ~224s total — similar headroom concern
- When the server queue is backed up with multiple concurrent submissions, the JIT time can push the total over the 12-minute limit
- Submissions that appear to fail silently (no output, cancelled status) are often **queue pressure timeouts**, not code errors

**Mitigation:** Retry submissions during off-peak hours. JIT results are cached between runs in the same workflow, so a retry that hits a pre-warmed runner will be much faster.

## Local Testing Limitations on gfx1151 (RDNA4 Strix Halo)

Local GPU testing on gfx1151 (AMD Radeon 8060S, Strix Halo iGPU) is NOT feasible with ROCm 6.2.4:

- Without override: `HIP error: invalid device function` (ISA mismatch)
- With `HSA_OVERRIDE_GFX_VERSION=11.0.0`: "Memory access fault by GPU node-2" on kernel launch
  (RDNA4 gfx1151 is not binary-compatible with RDNA3 gfx1100)
- ROCm 6.3+ would be needed for gfx1151 native support

**Workaround**: All testing via Popcorn CLI remote submission (MI355X hardware).

## `uv run` Reverting ROCm Torch Install

The project's uv lockfile pins CUDA torch (`torch+cu128`). Running `uv run` syncs from the
lockfile and overwrites any manually installed ROCm torch.

**Fix**: Create an isolated venv outside the project for GPU kernel work:

```bash
# Create isolated ROCm venv
python3 -m venv research/challenges/luma_amd_speedrun/.venv-rocm
source research/challenges/luma_amd_speedrun/.venv-rocm/bin/activate
pip install torch --index-url https://download.pytorch.org/whl/rocm6.2.4

# Use this venv directly (not uv run) for kernel testing
python submission.py
```

## Competition Kernel Structure

Each kernel must implement:
```python
# submission.py
from task import input_t, output_t

def custom_kernel(data: input_t) -> output_t:
    ...  # Your implementation
```

The eval harness (`eval.py`) calls `generate_input()` from `reference.py`, passes `_clone_data(data)`
to `custom_kernel`, then runs `check_implementation()` comparing output to reference. Benchmark
runs clear L2 cache between runs (`clear_l2_cache_large()`), so cache warming tricks don't help.

**CRITICAL: Popcorn CLI only uploads `submission.py`.** It does NOT upload any other files
in the kernel directory. Files like `reference.py`, `task.py`, and `eval.py` are already
present on the runner at the competition's standard location — they are NOT re-uploaded.

**Consequence: Multi-file submissions are NOT possible.** If your submission.py tries to
`from gemm_wrapper import quant_and_gemm` where `gemm_wrapper.py` is a file you created
in the kernel directory, the runner will get `ModuleNotFoundError: No module named 'gemm_wrapper'`.

**Workaround attempted — dynamic module creation at `/tmp/`:**
```python
# submission.py
import importlib.util, textwrap, os, sys

code = textwrap.dedent("""...""")
path = "/tmp/my_module.py"
with open(path, "w") as f: f.write(code)
spec = importlib.util.spec_from_file_location("my_module", path)
mod = importlib.util.module_from_spec(spec)
spec.loader.exec_module(mod)
sys.modules["my_module"] = mod
```

This WORKS for making the module importable, BUT for AMD `gemm_a4w4_asm` specifically,
the dynamic module does NOT change callsite identity — the dispatch still produces wrong
results (see `amd-triton-jit-callsite-correctness` skill). Only `from reference import
ref_kernel` reliably bypasses the callsite sensitivity.

## Troubleshooting: Transient Artifact Download Failures

Popcorn CLI submissions run on GitHub Actions runners. The evaluation job and the artifact
download are separate steps — a network failure can cause the CLI to report failure even though
the evaluation job itself **succeeded**.

**Symptoms:**
- CLI output shows failure / no results despite a correct kernel
- Error mentions artifact download or GitHub Actions artifact retrieval

**Fix:** Simply retry the same submission command. A retry succeeds in the majority of cases.

```bash
# Retry exactly the same command — the eval job is idempotent
$CLI submit --no-tui --mode benchmark --gpu MI355X --leaderboard <name> <file>
```

**Important:** Transient failures look identical to actual correctness failures in the CLI output.
If a previously-passing kernel suddenly "fails", try a retry before debugging the kernel code.

## Correctness Tolerances (Luma AMD Speedrun)

| Kernel | rtol | atol |
|--------|------|------|
| MXFP4 GEMM | 1e-2 | 1e-2 |
| MLA Decode | 1e-2 | 1e-2 |
| MoE MXFP4 | 5e-2 | 5e-2 |

## Test vs Benchmark Shape Differences (MLA-specific)

**Critical:** `task.yml` test shapes and benchmark shapes differ in `qseqlen`:

| Mode | qseqlen values | Why |
|------|---------------|-----|
| Test (`--mode test`) | May include qseqlen=4 | Exercises prefill-like cases |
| Benchmark (`--mode benchmark`) | Always qseqlen=1 | Decode-only benchmark |

Custom kernels must handle qseqlen>1 correctly or the test will fail, even if the
benchmark optimization only targets qseqlen=1. Safe pattern:

```python
total_q = q.shape[0]
qseqlen = total_q // bs
if qseqlen != 1:
    from reference import ref_kernel
    return ref_kernel(data)
# ... optimized decode path
```

**Observation (MLA, March 2026):** The test runner used only qseqlen=1 shapes in practice,
but the guard is still important for correctness robustness.

# gfx950 VGPR Pressure in Triton Kernels

## Problem

Triton kernels on AMD gfx950 (MI355X) perform much worse than expected for large tile
sizes. A FlashDecoding MLA kernel with `acc[32, 512]` fp32 accumulator ran 10x slower
than expected on large shapes, despite correct results.

## Root Cause: KV Tile Dominates VGPR Budget

On gfx950 with MFMA-16x16 layout:

| Tensor | Shape | VGPRs per thread | Notes |
|--------|-------|-------------------|-------|
| `acc[32, 512]` fp32 | 32x512 | ~256 | Saturates entire budget |
| `acc[16, 512]` fp32 | 16x512 | ~128 | Half budget (BLOCK_M=16) |
| `kv1[64, 512]` bf16 | 64x512 | ~256 | **The real bottleneck** |
| `q1[16, 512]` bf16 | 16x512 | ~64 | Persists across iterations |

**Key finding:** Reducing BLOCK_M from 32 to 16 halved the accumulator (256 → 128 VGPRs)
but did NOT improve performance because `kv1[BLOCK_N=64, D1=512]` alone requires ~256
VGPRs during `tl.dot`. The KV tile is the dominant register consumer, not the accumulator.

## BLOCK_M Tiling Increases KV Bandwidth

With `nq` heads and BLOCK_M tiling:
- `num_head_blocks = nq / BLOCK_M` blocks per batch element
- Each head block independently loads the same KV data
- Total KV bandwidth multiplied by `nq / BLOCK_M`

For MLA with nq=32, BLOCK_M=16: 2x KV bandwidth vs BLOCK_M=32.
For bs=256, kv=8192: 2 × 2.3 GB = 4.6 GB KV reads → 3900 µs (vs 370 µs with ref_kernel).

**Rule:** BLOCK_M tiling trades register pressure for bandwidth. Only beneficial when
shapes are compute-bound (small KV). For bandwidth-bound shapes (large KV), BLOCK_M
tiling is strictly worse.

## VGPR Budget Calculation (gfx950 MFMA-16x16)

Per-thread VGPR count for a 2D tensor in MFMA layout:
```
VGPRs = (dim0 / 16) × (dim1 / 16) × 4 values × (bytes_per_elem / 4)
```

For fp32 (4 bytes): `VGPRs = (M/16) × (N/16) × 4`
For bf16 (2 bytes): `VGPRs = (M/16) × (N/16) × 2` (packed into VGPRs)

gfx950 limit: 256 VGPRs per thread. Exceeding this causes spilling to scratch memory
(backed by HBM), which is orders of magnitude slower.

## Optimal Strategy for MLA FlashDecoding

| Shape regime | Bottleneck | Best approach |
|-------------|-----------|---------------|
| bs*kv ≤ 100k | Kernel launch + Python overhead | Custom FlashDecoding (avoids ref_kernel's ~130 µs fixed overhead) |
| bs*kv > 100k | HBM bandwidth | ref_kernel with fp8 KV (half bandwidth) |

Custom Triton FlashDecoding with split-D (512+64) wins for small shapes because:
1. No ~130 µs aiter metadata/dispatch overhead
2. Head-packed GEMM (M=16 or M=32, not M=1 GEMV)
3. FlashDecoding split-KV for CU utilization

## Verification

Benchmark both paths and set threshold empirically:
```python
if bs * kvseqlen > THRESHOLD:
    return ref_kernel(data)  # fp8 ASM, bandwidth-optimal
return _mla_flash_attention(q, kv_data, bs, kvseqlen, config)
```

## References

- MI355X: 304 CUs, 8 XCDs, 256 VGPRs/thread, ~8 TB/s HBM3e
- MFMA-16x16: 4 result elements per thread per tile
- aiter mla_decode_fwd: ~130 µs fixed overhead (metadata + JIT dispatch)

     ```python
     EINSUM_THRESHOLD = 100_000
     _cache: dict = {}
     
     def custom_kernel(data: input_t) -> output_t:
         # ... unpack data ...
         if qseqlen != 1:
             from reference import ref_kernel
             return ref_kernel(data)
         total_tokens = bs * kvseqlen
         if total_tokens <= EINSUM_THRESHOLD:
             # bf16 einsum path
             kv = kv_data["bf16"].view(bs, kvseqlen, 576)
             q_r = q.view(bs, nheads, 576)
             scores = torch.einsum("bnh,bsh->bns", q_r, kv) * SM_SCALE
             weights = torch.softmax(scores, dim=-1)
             v = kv[:, :, :V_HEAD_DIM]
             out = torch.einsum("bns,bsd->bnd", weights, v)
             return out.to(torch.bfloat16)
         # Large: mla_decode_fwd with cached metadata
         # ... build cache, quantize fp8, call mla_decode_fwd ...
     ```

   - **`kernels/mixed-mla/submission_triton_flash.py`** (NEW - Triton FlashDecoding variant)
     - Replaces einsum with Triton kernel using element-wise mul + reduce for small shapes
     - QK_HEAD_DIM=576 padded to QK_PAD=1024 to satisfy Triton's power-of-2 requirement
     - PASSED test with errors [5.9e-05, 7.1e-05, 0.0, 0.0] — awaiting benchmark
     ```python
     @triton.jit
     def _mla_flash_kernel(
         Q_ptr, KV_ptr, Out_ptr,
         stride_qb, stride_qh, stride_qd,
         stride_kvb, stride_kvs, stride_kvd,
         stride_ob, stride_oh, stride_od,
         sm_scale, kvseqlen,
         BLOCK_N: tl.constexpr, V_DIM: tl.constexpr,
         QK_REAL: tl.constexpr, QK_PAD: tl.constexpr,
     ):
         bid = tl.program_id(0)
         hid = tl.program_id(1)
         offs_qk = tl.arange(0, QK_PAD)
         qk_mask = offs_qk < QK_REAL
         q = tl.load(Q_ptr + bid * stride_qb + hid * stride_qh + offs_qk,
                     mask=qk_mask, other=0.0).to(tl.float32)
         # ... online softmax FlashDecoding loop ...
     ```
     - Hybrid: Triton kernel for ≤100k tokens, mla_decode_fwd for >100k

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - **MI355X (gfx950)**: AMD GPU, 304 CUs, 8 XCDs, 256 VGPRs/thread, ~8 TB/s HBM3e
   - **MLA (Multi-head Latent Attention)**: DeepSeek R1 forward_absorb path. KV has 576 dims (512 value + 64 rope). 16-32 query heads share 1 KV head (MQA/GQA).
   - **SDPA (`F.scaled_dot_product_attention`)**: PyTorch's built-in attention with `enable_gqa=True`. On AMD ROCm, dispatches to Composable Kernel (CK) flash attention — potentially the fastest path.
   - **Adaptive num_kv_splits**: `splits=1` for ≤8192 tokens skips reduce kernel entirely; `splits=8/16/32` for larger shapes
   - **a16w8 dtype routing**: bf16 Q + fp8 KV for small shapes (avoids Q quantization ~5µs overhead)
   - **Direct ASM calls**: Bypassing `mla_decode_fwd` Python wrapper, calling `stage1_asm_fwd` + `reduce_v1` directly with pre-allocated split buffers
   - **Popcorn CLI submission**: test → benchmark → leaderboard workflow, 12-min workflow timeout, 3 concurrent server limit
   - **aiter JIT build times**: module_mla_metadata (~32s), module_mla_asm (~21s), module_mla_reduce (~170s)
   - **qseqlen=4 in benchmarks**: Critical discovery — benchmark shapes include qseqlen=4, not just qseqlen=1. Half of benchmark shapes use qseqlen=4.
   - **Git worktrees**: Used for session isolation to avoid conflicting with other Claude/Gemini/OpenCode sessions

     ```python
     """
     MLA decode: SDPA with enable_gqa — uses CK flash attention on AMD.
     Handles both qseqlen=1 (decode) and qseqlen=4 (prefill) natively.
     K uses all 576 dims, V uses first 512 dims.
     enable_gqa=True handles 16-32 query heads sharing 1 KV head.
     """
     import torch
     import torch.nn.functional as F
     from task import input_t, output_t

     SM_SCALE = 1.0 / (576 ** 0.5)
     V_HEAD_DIM = 512
     QK_HEAD_DIM = 576

     def custom_kernel(data: input_t) -> output_t:
         q, kv_data, qo_indptr, kv_indptr, config = data
         bs = config["batch_size"]
         kvseqlen = config["kv_seq_len"]
         qseqlen = config["q_seq_len"]
         nheads = config["num_heads"]

         kv = kv_data["bf16"].view(bs, kvseqlen, QK_HEAD_DIM)
         q_4d = q.view(bs, qseqlen, nheads, QK_HEAD_DIM).transpose(1, 2)
         k = kv.unsqueeze(1)
         v = kv[:, :, :V_HEAD_DIM].unsqueeze(1)

         out = F.scaled_dot_product_attention(
             q_4d, k, v, scale=SM_SCALE, enable_gqa=True,
         )
         return out.transpose(1, 2).reshape(-1, nheads, V_HEAD_DIM)
     ```

   - **`kernels/mixed-mla/submission_matmul_fused.py`** (CREATED in worktree, test submitted)
     - Uses torch.matmul with 4D broadcasting for GQA
     - Updated to handle qseqlen=4 (originally had qseqlen=1 guard)
     ```python
     def custom_kernel(data: input_t) -> output_t:
         q, kv_data, qo_indptr, kv_indptr, config = data
         bs = config["batch_size"]
         kvseqlen = config["kv_seq_len"]
         qseqlen = config["q_seq_len"]
         nheads = config["num_heads"]

         kv = kv_data["bf16"].view(bs, kvseqlen, QK_HEAD_DIM)
         q_4d = q.view(bs, qseqlen, nheads, QK_HEAD_DIM).transpose(1, 2)
         k_t = kv.unsqueeze(1).transpose(2, 3)
         scores = torch.matmul(q_4d, k_t).mul_(SM_SCALE)
         weights = torch.softmax(scores, dim=-1)
         v = kv[:, :, :V_HEAD_DIM].unsqueeze(1)
         out = torch.matmul(weights, v)
         return out.transpose(1, 2).reshape(-1, nheads, V_HEAD_DIM)
     ```

   - **`kernels/mixed-mla/submission_compiled_matmul.py`** (CREATED in worktree)
     - torch.compile(reduce-overhead) wrapping the matmul attention core
     - Uses HIP graphs internally to eliminate kernel launch overhead
     - Not yet tested

   - **`kernels/mixed-mla/submission_combined_optimal.py`** (CREATED, copied to worktree)
     - Three regimes: tiny (≤8k tokens) → matmul, small-medium (8k-256k) → a16w8 mla_decode_fwd, large (>256k) → a8w8 mla_decode_fwd
     - Includes adaptive num_kv_splits and direct ASM call attempt
     - Fixed einsum bug for qseqlen=4 → replaced with matmul
     - Not yet tested

   - **`kernels/mixed-mla/submission_adaptive_splits.py`** (Read — from another session)
     - Uses `_choose_num_kv_splits(total_kv)`: 1/8/16/32 based on total tokens
     - a16w8 dtype routing with threshold 262144
     - All shapes go through mla_decode_fwd (no einsum)

   - **`kernels/mixed-mla/submission_direct_asm.py`** (Read — from another session)
     - Bypasses mla_decode_fwd Python wrapper
     - Direct `mla_decode_stage1_asm_fwd` + `mla_reduce_v1` calls
     - Pre-allocated split buffers cached per shape

   - **`kernels/moe-mxfp4/submission_doweight_opus_optimal.py`** (CREATED)

     - Not yet tested
     ```python

     os.environ["AITER_USE_OPUS_MOE_SORTING"] = "1"
     # Shape-aware routing:
     # est_m >= 100: default tuned config

     ```

     - `TOTAL_NUM_HEADS = 128`, `NUM_KV_HEADS = 1`, `QK_HEAD_DIM = 576`, `V_HEAD_DIM = 512`

# Competitive Kernel Optimization: Beyond Parameter Tuning

## Problem

After exhausting all tunable parameters in a library (e.g., aiter's `fused_moe`), ranking
stays in the lower half of the leaderboard. The reference implementation is already
well-optimized; parameter sweeps either have no effect, cause correctness failures, or
degrade performance.

## Context: When to Use This

- All accessible API parameters have been tested (documented in `aiter-kernel-parameter-semantics`)
- Still ranked outside top 20 with >2x gap to leader
- Considering whether to abandon vs. continue the challenge
- Wanting to plan next iteration with higher expected value

## The Decision Hierarchy

### Level 1: Local Proxy (Low effort, immediate)

Before expensive remote submissions, build a local model of the performance space:

**Parse aiter's tuned CSV configs:**
```bash
cat /path/to/aiter/aiter/configs/tuned_fmoe.csv
cat /path/to/aiter/aiter/configs/model_configs/dsv3_fp4_tuned_fmoe.csv
```

These CSVs contain: `(n_experts, batch_size, d_hidden, d_expert, topk, block_m, ...)` → `(kernel_name, cycles)`.
Reading them reveals:
- Which tile configs the auto-tuner selects for which shapes
- Whether there are shape-specific configs that differ from the defaults
- Whether your benchmark shapes have dedicated tuned entries or fall back to defaults

**Roofline model estimate:**
```python
# For MXFP4 GEMM: theoretical peak = GPU_TFLOPS * efficiency
# Compare against actual timing to determine if compute-bound or memory-bound
bytes_per_token = d_hidden * sizeof(fp4)  # input
bytes_weights = d_expert * d_hidden * sizeof(fp4)  # per expert, per token
arithmetic_intensity = 2 * d_hidden * d_expert / bytes_weights  # FLOPs/byte
```

### Level 2: The "Two Builders" Pattern (Medium effort)

From the Anthropic VLIW take-home challenge (see `research/challenges/anthropic_challenge/`):

The winning approach used two separate kernel builders:
- **Builder A (Correctness Anchor):** Delegates to `ref_kernel` or matches reference exactly.
  Never changes. Used to verify correctness of Builder B.
- **Builder B (Custom Explorer):** Reimplements the same computation differently.
  Can be tested against Builder A as ground truth.

For AMD MoE, this means writing a **custom dispatch + GEMM pipeline** that:
1. Uses the same shuffled weights and scales (pre-computed by aiter)
2. Implements its own token sorting (potentially simpler than aiter's)
3. Calls CK GEMM kernels directly instead of through `fused_moe`

```python
# Template: Two Builders pattern for AMD kernel submission
from reference import ref_kernel  # Builder A: correctness anchor

def custom_kernel(data: input_t) -> output_t:
    # Try Builder B; fall back to Builder A on any exception
    try:
        return custom_builder(data)
    except Exception:
        return ref_kernel(data)  # Safety net during development
```

### Level 3: Custom HIP/ASM Kernel (High effort, highest impact)

What top leaderboard entries do:
- Write kernel in HIP C++ or assembly, compiled with `hipcc`
- Bypass the aiter library entirely for the hot path
- Use hardware-specific optimizations (e.g., MI355X matrix core layouts, gfx950 ISA)
- Typically requires familiarity with ROCm programming model

This is only worth pursuing if:
- You have HIP/CUDA experience
- The competition has days remaining (not hours)
- The performance gap justifies the implementation risk

## VLIW Cross-Pollination: Applicable Lessons

The Anthropic VLIW take-home (`research/challenges/anthropic_challenge/`) shares structural
parallels with AMD GPU kernel challenges:

| VLIW Concept | AMD Kernel Analog |
|---|---|
| Instruction slots (alu, valu, load) | GPU execution units (VALU, SALU, memory) |
| `VLIWPacker` data-dependency tracking | aiter's 2-stage pipeline dependency between gate+up GEMM and down GEMM |
| "Temporal Instruction Leakage" across barriers | `doweight_stage1=True` leaking weight mul across the SiLU activation barrier |
| N_VEC=28 window interleaving for latency hiding | `num_kv_splits` in MLA for hiding KV cache memory latency |
| `multiply_add` fusion optimization | CK `moe_ck2stages_gemm1_*MulABScaleShuffled*` kernel name encodes similar fusion |
| Local simulator (instant feedback) | Popcorn CLI remote (~2-10 min/iteration) |

**Key VLIW insight transferable to AMD:**
The VLIW challenge was NOT won by parameter tuning the packer. It was won by:
1. Understanding how the scheduler works internally (`last_write`, `last_read` tracking)
2. Discovering that barriers needed explicit data-dependency injection
3. Building a custom kernel generator that produced different instruction schedules

Equivalent for AMD: understand how aiter selects kernels (`get_2stage_cfgs()` in `fused_moe.py`),
what the tile configs mean, and build something that uses them differently.

## Iteration Budget Management

With ~2 min test + ~8-10 min benchmark per remote submission (Luma AMD Speedrun):

| Action | Submits needed | Expected outcome |
|---|---|---|
| Parse CSV configs locally | 0 | Shape-specific tile insights |
| Roofline analysis | 0 | Compute vs memory bound diagnosis |
| Swarm brainstorm | 0 | Novel directions inventory |
| Single parameter test | 1-2 | Usually negative (already exhausted) |
| Custom dispatch prototype | 3-5 | Medium risk, medium reward |
| Custom HIP kernel | 10+ | High risk, high reward |

**Rule:** If a parameter test requires >2 submits to verify, it's probably not worth it.
Invest those submits in Builder B prototypes instead.

## Swarm Brainstorm Template

When stuck, use `swarm_brainstorm.py` (see `research/challenges/anthropic_challenge/`) as
template for a multi-model debate. Useful prompts for AMD MoE:

- "What CK kernel configurations exist for MoE that aren't exposed through fused_moe's API?"
- "Can we reuse the pre-shuffled weight layout with a different dispatch algorithm?"
- "What does the token distribution look like across experts for bs=16 with 256 experts?
   Does the sparse pattern suggest a different algorithm?"

## Context: Luma AMD Speedrun Competition

- **Challenge:** `amd-moe-mxfp4`, `amd-mixed-mla`, `amd-mxfp4-mm`
- **Hardware:** AMD Instinct MI355X (gfx950, ROCm 7.1, Torch 2.10+rocm7.1)
- **Parameter space exhausted as of March 2026:** All aiter API knobs tested and documented
  in `aiter-kernel-parameter-semantics` skill
- **VLIW reference:** `research/challenges/anthropic_challenge/simple_builder.py` (winner),
  `optimizer.py` (advanced variant with multiply_add fusion and crown caching)

## Phase 2 Exhausted Paths (March 2026)

| Kernel | Approach | Outcome | Blocker |

| GEMM | `get_triton_quant(shuffle=True)` from submission.py | Wrong results | JIT call-site sensitivity |
| GEMM | `get_triton_quant(shuffle=False)` from submission.py | Wrong results | Same JIT issue |
| GEMM | `get_torch_quant(QuantType.per_1x32)` | Wrong results | Different rounding than triton_quant |
| GEMM | `gemm_afp4wfp4` with uint8 views | Wrong results | Inherits wrong quant from torch_quant |

| MoE | `cktile_moe_stage1/stage2` direct call | Not attempted | Same sorting setup required as fused_moe_2stages |
| MLA | MXFP4 KV cache via `mla_decode_fwd` | RuntimeError | ASM kernel: `head_size == KV.size(3)` fails for packed dims (576 → 288) |

## Phase 3 Findings (March 2026)

| Kernel | Approach | Outcome | Key Insight |
|--------|----------|---------|-------------|
| GEMM | JIT cache warmup via `ref_kernel` first | Wrong results (89-95% mismatch) | See Phase 4: root cause was unpatched quant bug, not callsite sensitivity |
| GEMM | `get_hip_quant` (HIP compiled C kernel) | Wrong results (81-95% mismatch) | hip_quant output layout doesn't match `gemm_a4w4` contract |
| MoE | Direct `fused_moe` call from submission.py | ✅ WORKS | 0.015625 max error; `fused_moe` handles quant internally |

**CONFIRMED: Direct `fused_moe` call from submission.py works (March 2026)**

Tested and confirmed: 0/524288 mismatches, max_err=0.015625 across all 3 test shapes.
Benchmark baseline: ~155 µs geomean (at API ceiling for MXFP4 MoE).

**MoE ceiling:** ~155 µs vs leader's 145 µs (1.07x gap). No further API improvements found.

## Phase 4: GEMM Correctness Breakthrough (March 2026)

**Root cause was NOT "JIT callsite sensitivity" — it was an UNPATCHED QUANTIZATION BUG.**

`get_triton_quant(QuantType.per_1x32)` dispatches to the unpatched `fp4_utils.py` kernel
(ROCm/aiter issue #974). The fix is `dynamic_mxfp4_quant` from `aiter.ops.triton.quant`
(PR #975 patched this path). See `amd-triton-jit-callsite-correctness` v1.4.0 for details.

| Kernel | Approach | Outcome | Key Insight |
|--------|----------|---------|-------------|
| GEMM | `dynamic_mxfp4_quant` + `gemm_a4w4` | ✅ 0.0 error, ~24.1 µs | Patched Triton quant bypasses buggy dispatch |
| GEMM | `gemm_a4w4` with `log2_k_split=N` | ❌ RuntimeError | Torch op schema is fixed — no `log2_k_split` kwarg |
| GEMM | `gemm_a4w4_asm` direct call | ❌ Kernel name not found | Only 2 pre-compiled `.co` files; name selection is internal |
| GEMM | CUDA/HIP graph replay | ❌ ~36 µs (+50% overhead) | `copy_()` for 3 static input tensors exceeds kernel time |

**GEMM performance ceiling:** ~24.1 µs vs leader's 9.7 µs (2.49x gap).
The bottleneck is quantization time (~10-13 µs per call), which equals the GEMM time itself.
To close the gap requires a **fused quant+GEMM kernel** — not achievable via public aiter API.

**Key lesson:** When 2+ independent approaches all fail with similar-magnitude errors, suspect
the shared downstream component (here: the quantization output being wrong), not the
infrastructure connecting them (here: wrongly blamed callsite dispatch mechanism).

**Current leaderboard status (March 2026):**

| Kernel | Our Time | Leader | Gap | Status |
|--------|----------|--------|-----|--------|
| GEMM (`amd-mxfp4-mm`) | ~24.1 µs | 9.7 µs | 2.49x | On leaderboard |
| MoE (`amd-moe-mxfp4`) | ~155 µs | 145 µs | 1.07x | At API ceiling |
| MLA (`amd-mixed-mla`) | ~199 µs → **~100 µs** | 4.3 µs | **~23x (was 46x)** | See Phase 5 |

---

## Phase 5: MLA Pipeline Bypass (March 2026)

**Key insight: aiter's 3-stage MLA pipeline has fixed overhead that dominates for small batches.**

aiter's `mla_decode_fwd` runs: metadata computation → split-K ASM kernel → parallel reduce.
This pipeline is optimized for large workloads but has ~100-150 µs of constant overhead
regardless of batch size. For small batches (bs=4), the actual compute is tiny — the
overhead is the bottleneck.

**Approach:** Bypass the pipeline entirely with `torch.einsum` for small decode workloads:

```python
# Torch-native: Q@K^T + softmax + @V, all bf16
kv = kv_data["bf16"].view(bs, kvseqlen, 576)
scores = torch.einsum("bnh,bsh->bns", q, kv) * SM_SCALE
weights = torch.softmax(scores, dim=-1)
out = torch.einsum("bns,bsd->bnd", weights, v)
```

**Hybrid routing strategy (optimal):**
- `bs * kvseqlen <= 400_000` tokens: torch-native (pipeline overhead > compute)
- `bs * kvseqlen > 400_000` tokens: ref_kernel/aiter fp8 ASM (bandwidth-bound, fp8 wins)

**Results (all qseqlen=1, fp8 reference tolerance rtol=2e-2, atol=8e-3):**

| Shape | Previous (aiter fp8) | Hybrid | Factor |
|-------|---------------------|--------|--------|
| bs=4, kv=1k | ~150 µs | **27.7 µs** | 5.4x |
| bs=4, kv=8k | ~169 µs | **40.5 µs** | 4.2x |
| bs=32, kv=1k | ~179 µs | **46.2 µs** | 3.9x |
| bs=32, kv=8k | ~206 µs | **171 µs** | 1.2x |
| bs=64, kv=1k | ~175 µs | **64.7 µs** | 2.7x |
| bs=64, kv=8k | ~241 µs | **245 µs** | ~tie |
| bs=256, kv=1k | ~196 µs | **178 µs** | 1.1x |
| bs=256, kv=8k | ~397 µs | **399 µs** | ~tie |
| **Geomean** | ~199 µs | **~100 µs** | **2x** |

**Key learnings:**
- `fav3_sage_mxfp4` (Triton SAGE Attention) can't be used for MLA — requires separate K/V
  with same head_dim; MLA's fused buffer (K=576, V=512) is fundamentally incompatible.
- Correctness with bf16 KV vs fp8 reference: max error 5e-05 (tolerance: 8e-03) — excellent.
- The bottleneck shifts from pipeline overhead to memory bandwidth at ~400k tokens.
- Torch einsum is 2x more bandwidth-hungry than fp8 ASM (bf16 vs fp8 KV cache).

**Ceiling reached:** The hybrid approach exhausts pure-torch improvements.
Further MLA speedups require a custom Triton kernel with MXFP4 KV cache (4x bandwidth
savings vs fp8, requires decomposing the fused KV buffer).

**Updated leaderboard status:**

| Kernel | Our Time | Leader | Gap | Status |
|--------|----------|--------|-----|--------|
| GEMM (`amd-mxfp4-mm`) | ~24.1 µs | 9.7 µs | 2.49x | At API ceiling |
| MoE (`amd-moe-mxfp4`) | ~155 µs | 145 µs | 1.07x | At API ceiling |
| MLA (`amd-mixed-mla`) | ~100 µs | 4.3 µs | ~23x | Hybrid on leaderboard |

---

## Phase 6: Custom tl.dot_scaled Cannot Beat gemm_afp4wfp4 (March 2026)

**Conclusion: A standard tiled Triton MXFP4 GEMM cannot outperform aiter's `gemm_afp4wfp4` on MI355X.**

Fully implemented and benchmarked a custom `tl.dot_scaled` kernel with:
- Correct MXFP4 scale layout (BLOCK_M=64, BLOCK_N=64, BLOCK_K=64 → SCALE_PER_BLOCK=4)
- `@triton.autotune` with 12 curated configs (BM/BN: 16/32/64/128, BK: 64/128)
- Corrected Origami XCD scheduling bug (see `tritonblas-origami-xcd-remapping-bug` skill)

| Approach | Geomean | vs gemm_afp4wfp4 |
|----------|---------|-----------------|
| `gemm_afp4wfp4` (aiter, current best) | ~20.6 µs | — |
| Custom tl.dot_scaled, fixed BK=128 | ~37.6 µs | **+83% slower** |
| Custom tl.dot_scaled, @triton.autotune 12 configs | ~34.6 µs | **+68% slower** |
| Competition leader | ~9.7 µs | 2.12x ahead |

**Why the gap persists:** `gemm_afp4wfp4` uses a persistent kernel with split-K (`skip_reduce=True`)
and dozens of per-shape configs from `AITER_TRITON_CONFIGS_PATH`. A standard tiled kernel with
autotune cannot replicate this — the persistent kernel amortizes kernel launch overhead across
the entire problem, while a tiled kernel pays full launch cost per block.

**Key learnings:**
- Confirmed correctness: custom `tl.dot_scaled` with `dynamic_mxfp4_quant` gives 0.0 max error
  (first proof this path is viable for AMD gfx950)
- Origami XCD remapping bug: `cdiv` in remapping formula creates non-bijective tile mapping
  for shapes where `total_tiles % NUM_XCDS != 0`. Tiles are skipped silently. Fix: use
  standard group-M swizzle. See `tritonblas-origami-xcd-remapping-bug` skill.
- `gemm_afp4wfp4` internal API: `skip_reduce=True` returns `[num_splits, M, N]` float32 partial
  results; `_get_config(M, N, K)` selects per-shape tile configs; configs stored in
  `AITER_TRITON_CONFIGS_PATH` (JSON files, per shape/dtype).
- **Custom Triton GEMM ceiling:** A fused quant+GEMM persistent Triton kernel remains the only
  realistic path to beating `gemm_afp4wfp4`. The A quantization (~10-13 µs) and GEMM (~7-10 µs)
  must be fused into a single kernel for competitive performance.

**Decision:** Restore `gemm_afp4wfp4` submission (20.6 µs geomean, 2.12x gap). Custom kernel
path requires a persistent fused kernel which is a multi-day implementation effort.

---

## Phase 7: API Exhaustion Confirmed — deepgemm, fmoe_g1u1, hipblaslt (March 2026)

All remaining speculative API paths probed and confirmed as dead ends.

### GEMM: `aiter.deepgemm` / `aiter.deepgemm_ck`

**Confirmed: grouped GEMM for MoE — NOT applicable for standalone MXFP4 GEMM.**

Signature via `inspect`:
```python
aiter.deepgemm(
    XQ,           # [M, K//2] fp4x2  — input
    WQ,           # [N, K//2] fp4x2  — weight
    Y,            # [M, N] bf16       — output (in-place)
    group_layout, # REQUIRED: [E, 2] int32 — expert token layout tensor
    x_scale,      # [M, K//32] fp8_e8m0
    w_scale,      # [E, N, K//32] fp8_e8m0 — per-expert scales!
)
```

`group_layout` is mandatory and has no default. It describes how tokens are partitioned
across experts — a concept that doesn't exist in standalone GEMM. `deepgemm_ck` is a thin
wrapper around this same function. **Cannot be used for `amd-mxfp4-mm`.**

### GEMM: `hipblaslt`

```python
import hipblaslt
print(dir(hipblaslt))  # → ['hipblaslt']
print(hipblaslt.hipblaslt.attrs)  # → []
```

No fp4 GEMM functions available. The `attrs` list is empty — no operations exposed at all.
Complete dead end.

### MoE: `fmoe_g1u1`

**Confirmed: JIT-wrapped `torch.ops.aiter` operator with hidden calling convention.**

`inspect.getsource` shows only the `wrapper_custom` pattern — the actual kernel dispatch
happens via `torch.ops.aiter` which hides the schema behind `(*args, **kwargs)`. No way to
determine the correct calling convention without reading the C++ registration code (not
accessible from Python).

### MoE: `cktile_moe_gemm1` / `cktile_moe_gemm2` — Full Signatures Revealed

These are the cktile path kernels (triggered when AITER_KSPLIT>0). Full signatures:

```python
cktile_moe_gemm1(
    XQ,                  # [sorted_tokens, K] — sorted input activations
    WQ,                  # [E, N, K] — weight
    Y,                   # [sorted_tokens, N] — output
    sorted_ids,          # [sorted_tokens] — token→expert mapping
    sorted_expert_ids,   # [num_blocks] — block→expert mapping
    max_token_ids,       # scalar int — total valid tokens
    topk,                # int
    n_padded_zeros=0, k_padded_zeros=0,
    topk_weight=None, x_scale=None, w_scale=None, exp_bias=None,
    activation=0,        # 0=SiLU, 1=Swiglu
    block_m=32,          # tile size (int, not constexpr)
    split_k=1,           # K-split factor
) -> torch.Tensor

cktile_moe_gemm2(...)    # same signature pattern
```

Note: uses `max_token_ids` (scalar) instead of `num_valid_ids` tensor as in `ck_moe_stage1/2`.
The `split_k` here is the same concept as `splitk` in `ck_moe_stage1/2`.

### MoE: `ck_moe_stage1` / `ck_moe_stage2` — Full Signatures Confirmed

These are the CK MXFP4-optimized path kernels (KSPLIT=0). Full signatures now confirmed:

```python
ck_moe_stage1(
    hidden_states, w1, w2,
    sorted_token_ids, sorted_expert_ids, num_valid_ids,
    out, topk,
    kernelName=None,     # str — optional pre-selected kernel name
    w1_scale=None, a1_scale=None,
    block_m=32,
    sorted_weights=None,
    quant_type=0,        # 0=per_1x32 MXFP4
    activation=0,        # 0=SiLU
    splitk=1,
    non_temporal_load=False,
    dst_type=None,
    is_shuffled=True,    # REQUIRED True for MXFP4 preshuffle path
) -> None  # writes to `out` in-place

ck_moe_stage2(...)  # same signature, w2_scale/a2_scale instead of w1_scale/a1_scale
```

### Performance Note: Probe Code Overhead

Removing `inspect.getsource` stderr dumps from GEMM submission improved small-shape timing
by ~2-3 µs (m=4: 19.3 µs → 17.3 µs). Leaderboard submissions should be clean of probe code.

|--------|----------|--------|-----|--------|
| GEMM (`amd-mxfp4-mm`) | ~20.8 µs (ranked) | 9.7 µs | 2.15x | `gemm_afp4wfp4` on leaderboard |

**All public Python API paths are exhausted.** Closing the remaining gaps requires:
- GEMM: Fused quant+GEMM persistent Triton kernel (fuse 10-13 µs A-quant into the GEMM)
- MoE: Custom `ck_moe_stage1/2` direct call with optimal tile configs (requires sorting setup)
- MLA: Custom Triton kernel with MXFP4 KV cache (4x bandwidth vs fp8, requires KV decomposition)

## Phase 9: Team-Parallel Custom Kernels — Both Regressed (March 2026)

Three-agent team (Lead + mla-dev + gemm-dev) worked in parallel. Both kernels regressed.
Do not retry these paths.

| Kernel | Approach | Geomean | vs. Existing | Root Cause |
|--------|----------|---------|--------------|------------|
| GEMM | `gemm_afp4wfp4` + CUDA graph (silent fail) | ~26 µs | **+25% regression** vs 20.8 µs | Graph capture silently failed; Triton inherently slower than ASM |
| MLA | FP8 FlashDecoding (BLOCK_N=32, host Q quant) | ~168 µs | **+68% regression** vs 100 µs | Triton dispatch overhead (~130 µs) = ref_kernel overhead; zero gain |

**Test results:** Both kernels PASSED 4/4 correctness tests (GEMM: 0.0 max error;
MLA: ≤1.5e-5). Correctness was sound; the performance path was wrong.

### GEMM: The silent graph failure trap

`try/except: pass` around `torch.cuda.graph()` is an optimization anti-pattern:
- If capture fails (e.g., Triton JIT inside context), exception is swallowed silently
- `_graph_cache` never populates; every call runs non-graphed Triton
- Non-graphed `gemm_afp4wfp4` is slower than `gemm_a4w4` at all shapes
- The submission shows regression with no indication of why

**Rule:** Always use `gemm_a4w4` (ASM). Never switch to `gemm_afp4wfp4` for graph acceleration.

### MLA: The ~130 µs Triton dispatch floor

The ~130 µs overhead is NOT unique to aiter's 3-stage MLA pipeline. It applies to ANY
Triton kernel dispatched from Python on this problem:
- aiter `mla_decode_fwd` (3-stage): ~130 µs
- Custom Triton FlashDecoding (fp8, BLOCK_N=32): ~130 µs
- torch.einsum (vendor hipBLAS GEMV): **27–46 µs** ← the only way to escape the floor

The overhead is Python-level dispatch + Triton JIT, not the attention algorithm.
Escape requires a single CK/ASM kernel with no Python dispatch overhead.

**Updated leaderboard (after Phase 9 — no change from Phase 7):**

| Kernel | Our Time | Leader | Gap | Status |
|--------|----------|--------|-----|--------|
| GEMM (`amd-mxfp4-mm`) | ~20.8 µs | 9.7 µs | 2.15x | `gemm_a4w4` on leaderboard |
| MoE (`amd-moe-mxfp4`) | ~155 µs | 145 µs | 1.07x | Adaptive KSPLIT on leaderboard |
| MLA (`amd-mixed-mla`) | ~100 µs | 4.3 µs | ~23x | torch.einsum hybrid on leaderboard |

Phase 9 kernels NOT submitted. Existing Phase 7 entries remain best.

---

## Phase 10: Metadata Caching + Graph Capture Definitively Benchmarked (March 2026)

Three independent paths explored. One improved (MLA caching), two confirmed regressions.

| Kernel | Approach | Geomean | vs. Existing | Verdict |
|--------|----------|---------|--------------|---------|
| GEMM | `gemm_a4w4` ASM + HIP graph | ~41 µs | **+78% regression** | copy_() overhead dominates even when graph capture succeeds |
| MoE | `AITER_USE_NT=1` (non-temporal loads) | ~155 µs | **0% change** | CK kernels reuse weights via L2; bypassing L2 is neutral |
| MLA | `get_mla_metadata_v1` buffer caching | **~75 µs** | **-25% improvement** | Metadata rebuild was ~25 µs constant overhead per call |

### GEMM: ASM Graph Capture Succeeds but Still Regresses

Phase 9 showed gemm_afp4wfp4 (Triton JIT) silently fails graph capture.
Phase 10 confirmed gemm_a4w4 (ASM .co) successfully captures — but +78% regression:

```
m=4:   without=17 µs, with=32 µs (+88%)
m=16:  without=25 µs, with=49 µs (+96%)  ← copy_() dominates
m=256: without=24 µs, with=37 µs (+56%)
```

**Root cause:** Three `copy_()` calls per invocation (A, B_shuffle, B_scale_sh) scale
with tensor size. For A: M×7168 fp16, the copy alone exceeds the 8-34 µs GEMM time.
**Rule:** Data movement IS the bottleneck — not kernel dispatch. Graphs only help if inputs
already reside in static GPU buffers. For MXFP4 GEMM, they never do.

### MoE: Non-Temporal Loads Are Neutral

`AITER_USE_NT=1` bypasses L2 cache for weight reads. Hypothesis: weights aren't reused,
so NT loads avoid polluting L2 with stale data. **Wrong hypothesis:**
CK kernel tiles ARE reused across tokens — multiple tokens map to the same expert block.
L2 reuse IS beneficial for MoE. NT loads remove this benefit but also remove the reload
cost — the effects cancel at ~155 µs. Zero change across all 6 shapes.

### MLA: Metadata Caching Saves 25%

`get_mla_metadata_info_v1` + `get_mla_metadata_v1` allocate and populate 8 work buffer
tensors (`work_meta_data`, `work_indptr`, `work_info_set`, `reduce_indptr`,
`reduce_final_map`, `reduce_partial_map`, `kv_indices`, `kv_last_page_len`) on every call.
These are deterministic for fixed `(bs, qseqlen, kvseqlen)` — safe to cache:

```python
_cache: dict = {}

def _build_cache(bs, qseqlen, kvseqlen, qo_indptr, kv_indptr) -> dict:
    # ... get_mla_metadata_info_v1 + get_mla_metadata_v1 calls ...
    return {"kv_indices": ..., "work_meta_data": ..., ...}  # 8 tensors

key = (bs, qseqlen, kvseqlen)
if key not in _cache:
    _cache[key] = _build_cache(...)
c = _cache[key]
mla_decode_fwd(..., work_meta_data=c["work_meta_data"], ...)
```

Also lowered the einsum↔fp8 threshold from 400k→100k tokens (tighter crossover point).
Combined result: large shapes improved 27-43% while small shapes remained at einsum speed.

### Updated Leaderboard (after Phase 10)

| Kernel | Our Time | Leader | Gap | Status |
|--------|----------|--------|-----|--------|
| GEMM (`amd-mxfp4-mm`) | ~23 µs | 9.7 µs | 2.4x | `gemm_a4w4` (ASM), API ceiling |
| MoE (`amd-moe-mxfp4`) | ~155 µs | 145 µs | 1.07x | Adaptive KSPLIT, API ceiling |
| MLA (`amd-mixed-mla`) | **~75 µs** | 4.3 µs | 17x | Metadata cache + hybrid routing |

Phase 10 MLA is the best-ever submission. GEMM and MoE remain at Phase 7 API ceilings.
Remaining gaps require CK/ASM custom kernels with no Python dispatch overhead.

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   The user's overarching request from the previous session was: "Keep trying until we have a top 10 finish, remember we have multiple sessions working on this. Make sure popcorn cli is silent before making submissions." This session continues optimizing the MLA (Multi-head Latent Attention) kernel for MI355X (gfx950) in the Luma AMD Speedrun competition. The user also previously requested session isolation via a dedicated git worktree to avoid conflicts with other sessions.

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - **MI355X (gfx950)**: AMD GPU, 304 CUs, 8 XCDs, 256 VGPRs/thread, ~8 TB/s HBM3e
   - **MLA (Multi-head Latent Attention)**: DeepSeek R1 forward_absorb path. K=576 dims (512 value + 64 rope), V=512 dims. 16 query heads share 1 KV head (nheads=16, tp=8 confirmed from runner)
   - **aiter mla_decode_fwd**: Three-stage pipeline (metadata → stage1 ASM → reduce). Fixed overhead dominates small shapes.
   - **a16w8 vs a8w8 ASM kernels**: a16w8 (`mla_a16w8_qh16_m16x4_n16x1_coex0_mask1_ps`) is FASTER than a8w8 (`mla_a8w8_qh16_qseqlen1_gqaratio16_ps`) for mid-range shapes despite bf16 Q being less compute-efficient than fp8.
   - **num_kv_splits tuning**: splits=4 for small shapes (4k-16k tokens) dramatically improves CU occupancy. bs=4 with splits=1 gives only 64 workgroups on 304 CUs; splits=4 gives 256.
   - **SDPA failure on MLA**: `F.scaled_dot_product_attention` with asymmetric K/V dims (576 vs 512) doesn't dispatch to CK flash attention on ROCm — falls back to O(n²) naive path. Catastrophically slow (up to 97.8 ms).
   - **Runner benchmark shapes differ from task.yml**: Runner uses seeds 4217,4220,5412,5415,1357,1360,9823,9826 with bs=4,32,64,256 (all qseqlen=1). Local task.yml has different seeds with bs=4,32,128 and qseqlen=4 in benchmarks.
   - **Competitive ceiling**: Leader at 4.3 µs. Requires CK/ASM custom kernels with no Python dispatch overhead. Current best is ~79 µs with aiter API.

3. Files and Code Sections:

   - **`kernels/mixed-mla/task.yml`** (Read — confirmed benchmark shapes)
     - Benchmark shapes in task.yml: bs=4,32,128 with mixed tp=4/8 and qseqlen=1/4
     - But ACTUAL runner benchmarks use: bs=4,32,64,256, ALL qseqlen=1, ALL nheads=16 (tp=8)
     - Test shapes include qseqlen=4 (for correctness validation)
     - `ranking_by: "geom"` — geometric mean across 8 shapes

   - **`kernels/mixed-mla/submission.py`** (Modified multiple times — CURRENT VERSION is three-regime hybrid)
     - Started as `from reference import ref_kernel as custom_kernel` (passthrough)
     - Evolved through: adaptive_splits → all-a8w8 → a16w8+tuned splits → matmul hybrid
     - **Current version (last written, NOT YET TESTED):**
     ```python
     """
     MLA decode: matmul/aiter hybrid + tuned splits + metadata caching.
     Three-regime routing:
     1. Small batch (bs*qseqlen <= 8, total_kv <= 65536): torch.matmul bf16
     2. Medium (total_kv <= 262144): aiter a16w8 with adaptive splits
     3. Large (total_kv > 262144): aiter a8w8 with high split counts
     """
     import torch
     from task import input_t, output_t
     from aiter.mla import mla_decode_fwd
     from aiter import dtypes as aiter_dtypes
     from aiter import get_mla_metadata_info_v1, get_mla_metadata_v1

     SM_SCALE = 1.0 / (576 ** 0.5)
     V_HEAD_DIM = 512
     NUM_KV_HEADS = 1
     QK_HEAD_DIM = 576
     PAGE_SIZE = 1
     FP8_DTYPE = aiter_dtypes.fp8
     BF16_DTYPE = torch.bfloat16

     A16W8_THRESHOLD = 262144
     MATMUL_MAX_Q = 8
     MATMUL_MAX_KV = 65536

     _cache: dict = {}

     def _choose_num_kv_splits(total_kv):
         if total_kv <= 2048:
             return 1
         if total_kv <= 16384:
             return 4
         if total_kv <= 65536:
             return 8
         if total_kv <= 524288:
             return 16
         return 32

     def _quantize_fp8(tensor):
         finfo = torch.finfo(FP8_DTYPE)
         amax = tensor.abs().amax().clamp(min=1e-12)
         scale = amax / finfo.max
         return (
             (tensor / scale).clamp(finfo.min, finfo.max).to(FP8_DTYPE),
             scale.float().reshape(1),
         )

     def _build_cache(bs, qseqlen, kvseqlen, nheads, q_dtype, kv_dtype,
                      num_kv_splits, qo_indptr, kv_indptr):
         total_kv = bs * kvseqlen
         kv_indices = torch.arange(total_kv, dtype=torch.int32, device="cuda")
         kv_last_page_len = (kv_indptr[1:] - kv_indptr[:-1]).to(torch.int32)
         info = get_mla_metadata_info_v1(
             bs, qseqlen, nheads, q_dtype, kv_dtype,
             is_sparse=False, fast_mode=True,
             num_kv_splits=num_kv_splits, intra_batch_mode=True,
         )
         wm, wi, wis, ri, rfm, rpm = [
             torch.empty(s, dtype=t, device="cuda") for s, t in info
         ]
         get_mla_metadata_v1(
             qo_indptr, kv_indptr, kv_last_page_len,
             nheads // NUM_KV_HEADS, NUM_KV_HEADS, True,
             wm, wis, wi, ri, rfm, rpm,
             page_size=PAGE_SIZE,
             kv_granularity=max(PAGE_SIZE, 16),
             max_seqlen_qo=qseqlen, uni_seqlen_qo=qseqlen,
             fast_mode=True,
             max_split_per_batch=num_kv_splits,
             intra_batch_mode=True,
             dtype_q=q_dtype, dtype_kv=kv_dtype,
         )
         return {
             "kv_indices": kv_indices,
             "kv_last_page_len": kv_last_page_len,
             "work_meta_data": wm, "work_indptr": wi, "work_info_set": wis,
             "reduce_indptr": ri, "reduce_final_map": rfm, "reduce_partial_map": rpm,
         }

     def custom_kernel(data: input_t) -> output_t:
         q, kv_data, qo_indptr, kv_indptr, config = data
         bs = config["batch_size"]
         kvseqlen = config["kv_seq_len"]
         qseqlen = config["q_seq_len"]
         nheads = config["num_heads"]
         total_q = bs * qseqlen
         total_kv = bs * kvseqlen

         # Regime 1: Small batch — matmul with 4D broadcast
         if total_q <= MATMUL_MAX_Q and total_kv <= MATMUL_MAX_KV:
             kv = kv_data["bf16"].view(bs, kvseqlen, QK_HEAD_DIM)
             q_4d = q.view(bs, qseqlen, nheads, QK_HEAD_DIM).transpose(1, 2)
             k_t = kv.unsqueeze(1).transpose(2, 3)
             scores = torch.matmul(q_4d, k_t).mul_(SM_SCALE)
             weights = torch.softmax(scores, dim=-1)
             v = kv[:, :, :V_HEAD_DIM].unsqueeze(1)
             out = torch.matmul(weights, v)
             return out.transpose(1, 2).reshape(-1, nheads, V_HEAD_DIM)

         # Regime 2/3: aiter mla_decode_fwd
         use_a16w8 = total_kv <= A16W8_THRESHOLD
         num_kv_splits = _choose_num_kv_splits(total_kv)
         kv_fp8, kv_scale = kv_data["fp8"]
         kv_4d = kv_fp8.view(kv_fp8.shape[0], PAGE_SIZE, NUM_KV_HEADS, kv_fp8.shape[-1])
         if use_a16w8:
             q_input = q
             q_scale = None
             q_dtype = BF16_DTYPE
         else:
             q_input, q_scale = _quantize_fp8(q)
             q_dtype = FP8_DTYPE
         key = (bs, qseqlen, kvseqlen, nheads, use_a16w8, num_kv_splits)
         if key not in _cache:
             _cache[key] = _build_cache(
                 bs, qseqlen, kvseqlen, nheads,
                 q_dtype, FP8_DTYPE, num_kv_splits,
                 qo_indptr, kv_indptr,
             )
         c = _cache[key]
         o = torch.empty(
             (q.shape[0], nheads, V_HEAD_DIM),
             dtype=torch.bfloat16, device="cuda",
         )
         mla_decode_fwd(
             q_input.view(-1, nheads, QK_HEAD_DIM), kv_4d, o,
             qo_indptr, kv_indptr,
             c["kv_indices"], c["kv_last_page_len"],
             qseqlen,
             page_size=PAGE_SIZE, nhead_kv=NUM_KV_HEADS,
             sm_scale=SM_SCALE, logit_cap=0.0,
             num_kv_splits=num_kv_splits,
             q_scale=q_scale, kv_scale=kv_scale,
             intra_batch_mode=True,
             work_meta_data=c["work_meta_data"],
             work_indptr=c["work_indptr"],
             work_info_set=c["work_info_set"],
             reduce_indptr=c["reduce_indptr"],
             reduce_final_map=c["reduce_final_map"],
             reduce_partial_map=c["reduce_partial_map"],
         )
         return o
     ```

   - **`kernels/mixed-mla/submission_adaptive_splits.py`** (Read from main tree — basis for optimization)
     - Original adaptive_splits: a16w8/a8w8 hybrid, splits=1/8/16/32, metadata caching
     - 89.2 µs ranked geomean when submitted as submission.py

   - **`kernels/mixed-mla/reference.py`** (Reference — provides kv_data with bf16/fp8/mxfp4 formats)
   
   - **`kernels/mixed-mla/submission_sdpa_optimal.py`** (Created in worktree — SDPA approach, CONFIRMED DEAD END)
     - SDPA test passed 4/4 but benchmark showed up to 97.8 ms — catastrophically slow
   
   - **`kernels/mixed-mla/submission_matmul_fused.py`** (Created in worktree — matmul approach)
     - Uses torch.matmul with 4D broadcasting for GQA
     - Test was still running during previous context loss

   - **Worktree path**: `/home/mike-anderson/dev/cohezion/.worktrees/opus-mla-optimization/research/challenges/luma_amd_speedrun/kernels/`

4. Errors and Fixes:
   - **Popcorn CLI path error**: `Application error: File not found: /mixed-mla/submission.py` when using variable `$KERNELS`. Fixed by using absolute path instead of variable.
   - **SDPA catastrophically slow**: Up to 97.8 ms for bs=256/kv=8192. Root cause: asymmetric K/V dims (576 vs 512) prevent CK flash attention dispatch on ROCm. No fix — approach abandoned.
   - **All-a8w8 regression**: Switching from a16w8 to a8w8 for all shapes hurt mid-range performance (bs=32/kv=1024 went from 32→58 µs). Root cause: the `mla_a16w8_qh16_m16x4_n16x1` ASM kernel is intrinsically faster than `mla_a8w8_qh16_qseqlen1` for those shapes. Fixed by reverting to a16w8 hybrid while keeping tuned splits.
   - **Max 16 splits no improvement**: Reducing from 32 to 16 splits for largest shapes (hoping to reduce split buffer I/O) had no effect (79.2 vs 79.0 µs). Reverted to 32.
   - **JSON parse errors**: Popcorn CLI output files contained raw CLI text with ANSI escape codes, not clean JSON. Fixed by reading files with `Read` tool instead of `json.load`.

5. Problem Solving:
   - **Benchmark shape discovery**: Found that the ACTUAL runner benchmark shapes differ from local task.yml. Runner uses bs=4,32,64,256 (all qseqlen=1) while task.yml specifies bs=4,32,128 with mixed qseqlen. All runner shapes use nheads=16 (tp=8).
   - **Progressive optimization**: Systematically tested 5 variants:
     1. Reference passthrough: ~83 µs (baseline)
     2. Adaptive splits (a16w8 hybrid, original splits): 89.2 µs ranked (slightly worse due to noise)
     3. All-a8w8 + tuned splits: 92.5 µs (regression — a16w8 ASM is faster)
     4. **a16w8 hybrid + tuned splits=4: 79.0 µs bench / 80.6 µs ranked ← BEST SUBMITTED**
     5. Max 16 splits: 79.2 µs (no improvement, reverted)
   - **Key optimization win**: splits=4 for total_kv 2k-16k dramatically improved bs=4/kv=1024 from 82→37 µs (55% faster) by increasing CU occupancy from 64→256 workgroups on 304 CUs.

# /learn - Online Learning System

**Extract reusable knowledge from this session into skills.** Evaluates what was learned, checks for existing skills, and creates new ones when valuable patterns are discovered.

---

## TABLE OF CONTENTS

| Phase       | Description                                            | Steps   |
| ----------- | ------------------------------------------------------ | ------- |
| **Phase 0** | Reference: triggers, quality criteria, skill structure | 0.1–0.4 |
| **Phase 1** | Evaluate: assess if knowledge is worth extracting      | 1.1     |
| **Phase 2** | Check Existing: search for related skills              | 2.1     |
| **Phase 3** | Create Skill: write the skill file                     | 3.1–3.2 |
| **Phase 4** | Quality Gates: final checklist before saving           | 4.1     |

**Quick Step Reference:**

- 0.1 Triggers, 0.2 Quality criteria, 0.3 What NOT to extract, 0.4 Skill structure
- 1.1 Self-evaluation questions
- 2.1 Search existing skills
- 3.1 Choose location, 3.2 Write SKILL.md
- 4.1 Final checklist

---

## PHASE 0: REFERENCE

### Step 0.1: Triggers

Invoke `/learn` after ANY task involving:

| Trigger                      | Example                                                          |
| ---------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Non-obvious debugging**    | Spent 10+ minutes investigating; solution wasn't in docs         |
| **Misleading errors**        | Error message pointed wrong direction; found real cause          |
| **Workarounds**              | Found limitation and creative solution                           |
| **Tool integration**         | Figured out how to use tool/API in undocumented way              |
| **Trial-and-error**          | Tried multiple approaches before finding what worked             |
| **Repeatable workflow**      | Multi-step task that will recur; worth standardizing             |
| **External service queries** | Fetched data from Jira, GitHub, Confluence, or other APIs        |
| **User-facing automation**   | Built something user will ask for again (reports, status checks) |

### Step 0.2: Quality Criteria

Skills must be:

- **Reusable**: Will help with future tasks (not just this instance)
- **Non-trivial**: Required discovery, OR is a valuable workflow pattern
- **Verified**: Solution actually worked, not theoretical

### Step 0.3: What NOT to Extract

- Single-step tasks with no workflow value
- One-off fixes unlikely to recur
- Knowledge easily found in official docs

### Step 0.4: Skill Structure

**Location:** `.claude/skills/[skill-name]/SKILL.md`

**Template:**

```markdown
---
name: descriptive-kebab-case-name
description: |
  [CRITICAL: This determines when the skill triggers. Include:]
  - What the skill does
  - Specific trigger conditions (exact error messages, symptoms)
  - When to use it (contexts, scenarios)
author: Claude Code
version: 1.0.0
---

# Skill Name

## Problem

[Clear description of the problem]

## Context / Trigger Conditions

[When to use - exact error messages, symptoms, scenarios]

## Solution

[Step-by-step solution]

## Verification

[How to verify it worked]

## Example

[Concrete example]

## References

[Links to documentation]
```

<details>
<summary>Writing Effective Descriptions</summary>

The description field is CRITICAL for skill discovery:

**Good:**

```yaml
description: |
  Fix for "ENOENT: no such file or directory" errors in npm monorepos.
  Use when: (1) npm run fails with ENOENT, (2) paths work in root but
  not in packages, (3) symlinked dependencies cause failures.
```

**Bad:**

```yaml
description: Helps with npm problems in monorepos.
```

</details>

**Guidelines:**

- **Concise** - Claude is smart; only add what it doesn't know
- **Under 500 lines** - Move large docs to `references/`
- **Examples over explanations** - Show, don't tell

---

## PHASE 1: EVALUATE

### Step 1.1: Self-Evaluation Questions

Ask yourself:

1. "What did I just learn that wasn't obvious before starting?"
2. "Would future-me benefit from having this documented?"
3. "Was the solution non-obvious from documentation alone?"
4. "Is this a multi-step workflow I'd repeat on similar tasks?"
5. "Did I query an external service the user will ask about again?"

**If NO to all → Skip extraction, nothing to learn.**

**Note:** External service queries (Jira tickets, GitHub PRs, Confluence pages) are almost always worth extracting - users frequently repeat these requests.

---

## PHASE 2: CHECK EXISTING

### Step 2.1: Search for Related Skills

Before creating, search for related skills:

```bash
ls .claude/skills/ 2>/dev/null
rg -i "keyword" .claude/skills/ 2>/dev/null
ls ~/.claude/pilot/skills/ 2>/dev/null
rg -i "keyword" ~/.claude/pilot/skills/ 2>/dev/null
```

| Found                | Action                           |
| -------------------- | -------------------------------- |
| Nothing related      | Create new skill                 |
| Same trigger and fix | Update existing (bump version)   |
| Partial overlap      | Update existing with new variant |

## PHASE 3: CREATE SKILL

### Step 3.1: Choose Location

**Project skills**: `.claude/skills/[skill-name]/SKILL.md`

### Step 3.2: Write SKILL.md

Use the template from Step 0.4. Ensure the description field contains specific trigger conditions.

## PHASE 4: QUALITY GATES

### Step 4.1: Final Checklist

Before finalizing:

- [ ] Description contains specific trigger conditions
- [ ] Solution verified to work
- [ ] Content specific enough to be actionable
- [ ] Content general enough to be reusable
- [ ] No sensitive information

## REMEMBER

**Continuous improvement.** Every valuable discovery should benefit future sessions.
Evaluate after significant work. Extract selectively. Create carefully.

---

This session is being continued from a previous conversation that ran out of context. The summary below covers the earlier portion of the conversation.

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   The user's overarching request (from previous context) was: "Keep trying until we have a top 10 finish, remember we have multiple sessions working on this. Make sure popcorn cli is silent before making submissions." This session continued optimizing the MLA (Multi-head Latent Attention) kernel for the Luma AMD Speedrun competition on MI355X (gfx950). The user issued `/learn` at the end to extract session knowledge into reusable skills.

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - **MI355X (gfx950)**: AMD GPU, 304 CUs, ~8 TB/s HBM3e, ROCm 7.1
   - **MLA decode**: DeepSeek R1 absorbed formulation. Q: `(bs, qseqlen, nheads, 576)`, KV: `(total_kv, 1, 576)` fused buffer. V sliced as `KV[:,:512]`. Output: `(total_q, nheads, 512)`
   - **aiter pipeline overhead**: `mla_decode_fwd` has ~25 µs constant overhead (metadata + ASM dispatch + reduce) regardless of batch size. Dominates small-batch shapes.
   - **torch.einsum vs torch.matmul**: `torch.einsum("bqnh,bsh->bnqs", q_r, kv)` dispatches to optimized hipBLAS GEMV kernels. 4D matmul with view/transpose/unsqueeze creates catastrophic overhead.
   - **a16w8 vs a8w8 ASM kernels**: `mla_a16w8_qh16_m16x4_n16x1` faster for mid-range shapes; `mla_a8w8_qh16_qseqlen1_gqaratio16` better for bandwidth-bound large shapes
   - **num_kv_splits tuning**: Split counts control CU occupancy; too many splits (64) exceed aiter metadata API limits
   - **kv_granularity**: 16 is optimal; 32 causes +4.4% regression
   - **Metadata caching**: `get_mla_metadata_v1` work buffers are deterministic for fixed shapes — cached to save ~25 µs
   - **Geometric mean ranking**: Competition uses `ranking_by: "geom"` over 8 benchmark shapes
   - **Benchmark shapes (actual runner)**: bs=4,32,64,256 × kv=1024,8192, ALL qseqlen=1, ALL nheads=16

3. Files and Code Sections:
   - **`kernels/mixed-mla/submission.py`** (primary file, modified throughout session)
     - Final version on leaderboard (69.5 µs ranked geomean):
     ```python
     """
     MLA decode: einsum/aiter hybrid + tuned splits + metadata caching.

     Three-regime routing:
     1. Small (bs<=4): torch.einsum bf16 (bypasses aiter pipeline overhead)
     2. Medium (total_kv <= 262144): aiter a16w8 with adaptive splits
     3. Large (total_kv > 262144): aiter a8w8 with high split counts
     """
     import torch
     from task import input_t, output_t
     from aiter.mla import mla_decode_fwd
     from aiter import dtypes as aiter_dtypes
     from aiter import get_mla_metadata_info_v1, get_mla_metadata_v1

     SM_SCALE = 1.0 / (576 ** 0.5)
     V_HEAD_DIM = 512
     NUM_KV_HEADS = 1
     QK_HEAD_DIM = 576
     PAGE_SIZE = 1
     FP8_DTYPE = aiter_dtypes.fp8
     BF16_DTYPE = torch.bfloat16

     A16W8_THRESHOLD = 262144
     EINSUM_MAX_TOTAL_KV = 65536
     EINSUM_MAX_BS = 4

     _cache: dict = {}

     def _choose_num_kv_splits(total_kv):
         if total_kv <= 2048:
             return 1
         if total_kv <= 16384:
             return 4
         if total_kv <= 131072:   # was 65536 in previous version
             return 8
         if total_kv <= 524288:
             return 16
         return 32

     def _quantize_fp8(tensor):
         finfo = torch.finfo(FP8_DTYPE)
         amax = tensor.abs().amax().clamp(min=1e-12)
         scale = amax / finfo.max
         return (
             (tensor / scale).clamp(finfo.min, finfo.max).to(FP8_DTYPE),
             scale.float().reshape(1),
         )

     def _build_cache(bs, qseqlen, kvseqlen, nheads, q_dtype, kv_dtype,
                      num_kv_splits, qo_indptr, kv_indptr):
         total_kv = bs * kvseqlen
         kv_indices = torch.arange(total_kv, dtype=torch.int32, device="cuda")
         kv_last_page_len = (kv_indptr[1:] - kv_indptr[:-1]).to(torch.int32)
         info = get_mla_metadata_info_v1(
             bs, qseqlen, nheads, q_dtype, kv_dtype,
             is_sparse=False, fast_mode=True,
             num_kv_splits=num_kv_splits, intra_batch_mode=True,
         )
         wm, wi, wis, ri, rfm, rpm = [
             torch.empty(s, dtype=t, device="cuda") for s, t in info
         ]
         get_mla_metadata_v1(
             qo_indptr, kv_indptr, kv_last_page_len,
             nheads // NUM_KV_HEADS, NUM_KV_HEADS, True,
             wm, wis, wi, ri, rfm, rpm,
             page_size=PAGE_SIZE,
             kv_granularity=max(PAGE_SIZE, 16),
             max_seqlen_qo=qseqlen, uni_seqlen_qo=qseqlen,
             fast_mode=True,
             max_split_per_batch=num_kv_splits,
             intra_batch_mode=True,
             dtype_q=q_dtype, dtype_kv=kv_dtype,
         )
         return {
             "kv_indices": kv_indices,
             "kv_last_page_len": kv_last_page_len,
             "work_meta_data": wm, "work_indptr": wi, "work_info_set": wis,
             "reduce_indptr": ri, "reduce_final_map": rfm, "reduce_partial_map": rpm,
         }

     def custom_kernel(data: input_t) -> output_t:
         q, kv_data, qo_indptr, kv_indptr, config = data
         bs = config["batch_size"]
         kvseqlen = config["kv_seq_len"]
         qseqlen = config["q_seq_len"]
         nheads = config["num_heads"]
         total_kv = bs * kvseqlen

         # Regime 1: Small batch — einsum (bypasses aiter pipeline overhead)
         if bs <= EINSUM_MAX_BS and total_kv <= EINSUM_MAX_TOTAL_KV:
             kv = kv_data["bf16"].view(bs, kvseqlen, QK_HEAD_DIM)
             q_r = q.view(bs, qseqlen, nheads, QK_HEAD_DIM)
             scores = torch.einsum("bqnh,bsh->bnqs", q_r, kv).mul_(SM_SCALE)
             weights = torch.softmax(scores, dim=-1)
             v = kv[:, :, :V_HEAD_DIM]
             out = torch.einsum("bnqs,bsd->bqnd", weights, v)
             return out.reshape(-1, nheads, V_HEAD_DIM)

         # Regime 2/3: aiter mla_decode_fwd
         use_a16w8 = total_kv <= A16W8_THRESHOLD
         num_kv_splits = _choose_num_kv_splits(total_kv)
         kv_fp8, kv_scale = kv_data["fp8"]
         kv_4d = kv_fp8.view(kv_fp8.shape[0], PAGE_SIZE, NUM_KV_HEADS, kv_fp8.shape[-1])
         if use_a16w8:
             q_input = q
             q_scale = None
             q_dtype = BF16_DTYPE
         else:
             q_input, q_scale = _quantize_fp8(q)
             q_dtype = FP8_DTYPE
         key = (bs, qseqlen, kvseqlen, nheads, use_a16w8, num_kv_splits)
         if key not in _cache:
             _cache[key] = _build_cache(
                 bs, qseqlen, kvseqlen, nheads,
                 q_dtype, FP8_DTYPE, num_kv_splits,
                 qo_indptr, kv_indptr,
             )
         c = _cache[key]
         o = torch.empty(
             (q.shape[0], nheads, V_HEAD_DIM),
             dtype=torch.bfloat16, device="cuda",
         )
         mla_decode_fwd(
             q_input.view(-1, nheads, QK_HEAD_DIM), kv_4d, o,
             qo_indptr, kv_indptr,
             c["kv_indices"], c["kv_last_page_len"],
             qseqlen,
             page_size=PAGE_SIZE, nhead_kv=NUM_KV_HEADS,
             sm_scale=SM_SCALE, logit_cap=0.0,
             num_kv_splits=num_kv_splits,
             q_scale=q_scale, kv_scale=kv_scale,
             intra_batch_mode=True,
             work_meta_data=c["work_meta_data"],
             work_indptr=c["work_indptr"],
             work_info_set=c["work_info_set"],
             reduce_indptr=c["reduce_indptr"],
             reduce_final_map=c["reduce_final_map"],
             reduce_partial_map=c["reduce_partial_map"],
         )
         return o
     ```

   - **`~/.claude/skills/deepseek-mla-decode-flash-attention-gap/SKILL.md`** (read during /learn phase)
     - Existing skill covering Phase 5–7 MLA findings. Needs Phase 11 update.
     - Current best listed as ~97 µs; actual best is now 69.5 µs ranked.

   - **`~/.claude/skills/competitive-kernel-optimization-ceiling/SKILL.md`** (loaded in session context)
     - Covers Phases 1–10. Needs Phase 11 (this session's) findings added.

4. Errors and Fixes:
   - **torch.matmul 4D broadcasting catastrophically slow**: Initial approach used `q.view(bs, qseqlen, nheads, QK_HEAD_DIM).transpose(1, 2)`, `kv.unsqueeze(1).transpose(2, 3)`, then `torch.matmul`. Result: 231 µs for bs=4/kv=1k (vs 37 µs with aiter). Root cause: view/transpose/unsqueeze creates temporary tensors and dispatches to suboptimal hipBLAS kernel. Fix: replaced with `torch.einsum("bqnh,bsh->bnqs", q_r, kv)` which dispatches to optimized GEMV path.
   - **Matmul threshold too aggressive (MATMUL_MAX_KV = 65536)**: From previous session's written code, threshold routed bs=4/kv=8k (32k tokens) to matmul, causing 1285 µs. Fix: tightened to `MATMUL_MAX_TOTAL_KV = 4096`, later changed to einsum approach.
   - **64 splits caused test failure**: Trying `return 64` for total_kv > 1M tokens caused popcorn-cli to return exit code 1 (test failure). Root cause: exceeds aiter metadata API limits. Fix: reverted to `return 32`.
   - **a16w8 threshold 524288 caused regression**: bs=64/kv=8k regressed from 154→170 µs when raising threshold, since a16w8 is slower than a8w8 for bandwidth-bound shapes at 512k tokens. Fix: reverted to A16W8_THRESHOLD = 262144.
   - **kv_granularity=32 caused regression**: bs=32/kv=1k regressed 31.1→33.8 µs, bs=64/kv=8k 145→159 µs (+4.4% geomean). Fix: reverted to `max(PAGE_SIZE, 16)`.
   - **Cached output tensor had no benefit**: `torch.empty` allocation is essentially free on GPU (no initialization), so caching it saved nothing. Reverted.

5. Problem Solving:
   - **Root cause of matmul slowness**: 4D tensor reshape + transpose + unsqueeze chain creates non-contiguous tensors with extra copies. `torch.einsum` avoids this by directly specifying the contraction pattern, which BLAS can dispatch as a single GEMV operation.
   - **Why einsum wins for bs≤4**: aiter's 3-stage pipeline has ~25 µs constant overhead for metadata setup. With only 4 batch elements and 16 heads = 64 workgroups on 304 CUs, the actual compute is tiny. Einsum dispatches directly to hipBLAS, bypassing all pipeline overhead.
   - **Why einsum loses for bs≥32**: At bs=32, aiter's pipeline overhead is amortized across more batch elements, and the split-K ASM kernel provides better CU utilization (512+ workgroups). Einsum for bs=32/kv=1k gives 46 µs vs aiter's 32 µs.
   - **Optimal threshold discovery**: `bs <= 4 AND total_kv <= 65536` captures both bs=4/kv=1k (4096 tokens, 23 µs) and bs=4/kv=8k (32768 tokens, 38 µs) for the einsum path. Widening to 65536 from 4096 was the session's biggest single improvement (87→38 µs for bs=4/kv=8k).
